ParlaMint-AT_1998-11-05-020-XX-NRSITZ-00146_d7e2440	Thank you, Mr President. I am concerned about the well-being of students and universities and not about reminiscences. Federal Minister! What are the basic strategies and requirements? In the discussion that is currently being held by the Sorbonne Declaration on the introduction of a Backalaureat in Austria, how can you prevent this discussion from leading to a delay in the curriculum reform in the outstanding fields of study?
ParlaMint-AT_1998-11-05-020-XX-NRSITZ-00146_d7e2459	Please, Minister of State.
ParlaMint-AT_1998-11-05-020-XX-NRSITZ-00146_d7e2474	Deputies! Let me introduce the answer to your question with a preliminary remark. The declaration, the four ministers of science in Europe, on the occasion of the 800th anniversary. 25th birthday of the Paris University Sorbonne. In May of this year, two aspects have, as it were, been signed. One aspect is a European policy, and it is not particularly pleasing. If the ministers of science of the four largest states have a good idea of putting it into practice immediately and then inviting the other ministers of science and saying that they could go into this boat too, then such an attitude is not exactly characterized by a particularly cooperative spirit within the meaning of the European Union and its citizens. In this respect, we have therefore also expressed our stranger – together with other countries that were not invited to participate in it from home. Second aspect: On the other hand, the scientific and university policy assumes that the direction taken in the Sorbonne Declaration is useful and useful. It is therefore useful and useful because it is now about creating something that could be called a European university: with comparable or similar degrees and with precisely planned interfaces, which allow students not only to change the university, but also the country, during their studies, without losing time. That is also fully in our intentions. It is also true that standardized diplomas, common, unified forms of diplomas, of course also contribute to the fact that graduates of a university degree in one of the European universities, but also at the American universities, find comparable conditions to find work in Europe, in all Member States. This too is fully included in our intention. So what will we do? – I also see the risk of a second reform, which is the implementation of the University Study Act, which is currently under way and with great commitment by the universities. There is a risk that everyone will turn back and say: Now let's see what's really coming. I therefore held a first detailed discussion with the Bureau of the Rectors' Conference early on, even before the beginning of the winter semester, and agreed that we should immediately set up a working group whose objective is essentially twofold. The first objective is to determine – as soon as possible indicatively – in which areas, from the university's point of view, the implementation of a three-stage study model with the Bakkalaureat appears to be useful, useful and rapid. The second objective is also to clarify the legal framework for this. In addition, thirdly, it will be necessary to carry out a very solid assessment of labour market opportunities for a Backalaureat degree, because our interest undoubtedly is not to create an intermediate degree, which ultimately leads to an extension of the study. If, then, a degree should be created, which is also qualified for professional activity and accepted by the economy or other employers, because otherwise we would provide the students with a bear service. We have recently discussed this question in detail at the rectors' conference and have asked for the implementation of the study plan to be carried out in the manner provided for by the law. We have also asked that, in areas where there is already an urgent need for threat, this need be followed and accompanied in parallel by the following measures: appropriate preparatory work, evaluation of labour market opportunities and the establishment of the conditions for a legislative proposal to be sent to the House as soon as possible.

However, allow me to hold where, in my opinion, there is a particular need for action in order to enable women who have been selected to enter employment or to re-enter employment: This is the legal right to part-time work, which is the better transparency between full-time and part-time work, and this is also the further development of unilateral legal claims for parents to benefit from benefits. That is why I think that the focus should be on this area.

Ladies and gentlemen! Through historical research, through the education and education that people receive at our schools and our high schools, the awareness of injustice is increasing because the crimes are becoming increasingly evident. If we decide today on these laws, which are just two small steps, we will face this increased awareness of responsibility. [[Congratulations with the ÖVP and with representatives of the SPÖ.]] What has been done to our Jewish fellow citizens by National Socialists and National Socialists, we cannot do again, but we must deal with it and do what we can to brand injustice as injustice and to alleviate the consequences of injustice. [[Congratulations with the ÖVP and with representatives of the SPÖ.]] Above all, and for all: Injustice cannot be justified by time. This applies not only to the victims of the Shoa, not only to the other victims of National Socialism, but to all. [[Congratulations with the ÖVP and with representatives of the SPÖ.]] Our Jewish fellow citizens were injured and robbed of body and life in the time of National Socialism. But even after the end of the Nazi terror, mistakes have been made in the young Second Republic, for which reasons always and by whomever, and we have to acknowledge this today as a member of an older Second Republic. Today, we are once again fulfilling our responsibilities and – I have already said it – we are taking two small steps. The first step is that we provide the equivalent of 100 million schilling for the hardships of victims of National Socialism, hard cases that fell through the meshes of the law. That's the one step. The other step is as follows: We give back art objects people who have been deprived of the National Socialism, art objects which were returned to these people after the war, but which remained parts of the Republic and were made the property of the Republic. Although the victims of this robbery were given their abandoned art objects, in order to export them abroad, they had to give parts of the Republic. At that time, we want to correct this legal but, according to our current understanding, we want to correct it. It should be noted that we are going through all our galleries and museums, all of which are owned by the Republic, whether there are objects of art which are dubious origin, which have been extorted, or which have remained in the country by using this so-called export ban, so that the legitimate owners may export other art objects abroad. [[President Dr. Brauneder shall take over the chairmanship.]] Mrs Bundesminister Gehrer has ordered the so-called Provenance Research, without being forced by anyone, to follow her ministerial responsibility and her inconsistency, to arrange a provenance research which concerns all the art collections in her administration, but also those in the administration of other ministries, in order to clarify their origin and then, if our law is adopted today, to restore these illegitimate objects of art to the owners or her heirs quickly and unbureaucratically. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] I would like to thank Mrs Gehrer and Mr Federal Minister for Finance Rudolf Edlinger, who, together with the clubs and with the National Council, prepared these laws very quickly, and who, in themselves, have discussed with us the difficult procedure for the return or the surrender of federal assets, where, each time, for each individual subject of their own law, it needs to be discussed with us and made possible by this law, which, while safeguarding the rule of law, allows for rapid investigation and rapid return of this unlawful good. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] I would also like to thank all the political groups, because these laws will be passed unanimously, following a tradition which we have founded when we set up the National Fund of the Republic, and where all the political groups, although many different opinions, have in principle agreed to this regulation. What exactly is happening, ladies and gentlemen, what is the gold, the raw gold? – The gold is not gold that was robbed of our Jewish fellow citizens, but it is gold that was taken from the stock of the Austrian National Bank in 1938. In 1946, ten countries were defined in the Washington Agreement, which can retain the Nazi gold of robbery. We got a first tranche of our gold very quickly after World War II, and now another tranche, the final billing, so to speak, and gold went to the Oesterreichische National Bank worth 102 million schilling. [[President Dr. Fischer is taking over the presidency again.]] Following an initiative from the United Kingdom and the United States, we have provided this gold to an international fund to the victims of the Shoa for the proceeds of this gold. We were the first to do this. The international fund is well founded. It will, in turn, provide this gold to our National Fund and we will distribute these funds beyond the means provided for by the Federal Budget for the victims of National Socialist Terror in Austria to particularly needy and harsh cases. This is a very good law, and I thank all those who have contributed. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] The second case is the initiative taken by Mrs Gehrer, supported by Minister Edlinger. Scheibner.]] Many in the population would have responded from the instinct and said: The pictures are legitimately acquired, they are not illegal things, you're on the pauke here! ParlaMint-AT_1998-11-05-020-XX-NRSITZ-00146_d7e6037	Mr President. High house! We are today adopting a law in Parliament on the return of art objects from the Austrian federal museums and collections. As I see it, the law is supported by all political groups. I mean, this law is a very important step at the end of this historicly burdened century. How has this law come to be? – In the 1990s, there was already a rework of the archive material on the subject of "Browing and restitution of art and cultural objects". A concrete occasion was then the retention of the Schiele images from the "Collection Leopold" in January 1998. I then had in January 1998 that all the archives of the Federal Museums and Collections and the Federal Monumental Office should be opened for systematic work. I have already invited the directors of all these institutions to me in January and discussed the procedure with them. I am, however, very grateful for the public reporting that has been carried out, and for all the technical articles that were later written, because they have contributed a lot to a positive opinion for this law. In March 1998, I set up the Commission, which coordinates provenance research at all federal museums and collections, and commissioned Chairman Ernst Bacher to coordinate the entire archival work, the entire work. At the same time, a working group has been set up which has initiated the drafting of this law. I would also like to mention that the Federal Constitutional Service in this working group has cooperated very concretely and very positively, that of course the questions of the Constitution have also been discussed there and that we have also been confirmed by this side that our law is constitutional. The main features of this law are: There are three categories. The first category is art items that were robbed during the war, restored after the war, but retained under the Export Prohibition Act. The second category is art, which, although legally acquired by the federal government, was the subject of a legal transaction under the law of invalidity. The third category is art and culture, which could not be returned in spite of the implementation of restitution procedures and has been transferred into the property of the federal government as an inventive property. So that means: If claims or alleged claims are present which are not covered by these three categories, they must be filed and dealt with on the normal legal basis. The law also has the cornerstone that an advisory board – consisting of representatives of the Federal Ministries of Justice, Economy, Education and Cultural Affairs and National Defense, a representative of the Financial Procuratorate and an expert in history and art history – will review the revision and then draw up recommendations for the return. The experts in history and art history are appointed by the rectors' conference. I will also send to Parliament a report on the implementation of this law every year. The return is also exempt from all restrictions of the Export Prohibition Act and from all charges. I think it is particularly important to keep clear: We have received these items free of charge and without evaluation, we will return them free of charge and without evaluation. If this law is adopted in Parliament today, I will immediately request all the institutions to appoint representatives of the Advisory Council in writing. I am very concerned that this advisory board will start working in the next few weeks and that we will be able to carry out the first return this year. [[Congratulations with the ÖVP and with representatives of the SPÖ.]] Ladies and gentlemen! I am not proud that we have needed 50 years for this law, but I am glad that there is now a broad consensus on a legal basis for the return of art goods to which the owners have a moral claim. It is not reparation, but it is a bit more justice that we want to show with this law. We will return the works of art to their owners or heirs. Austria thus sets for itself, but also beyond its borders, a signal to a new consciousness in the reworking of an uncanny part of its history. I would like to thank all those who have contributed with great intensity in recent months to the study of the provences in the federal museums and collections, and I thank all those who have contributed to the drafting of this law.

In any case, I believe that collegiality in this body – and this is another argument that has been mentioned over and over again – is so strong, and foreign examples confirm my view that a "disstinction opinion" does not slow down any decision-making inability – quite the contrary. The President of the German Federal Constitutional Court has expressly demonstrated to us that this has led to no difficulties in their country, but rather to an acceleration of the decision-making process in the final stage. Therefore, once again: The public, transparency and democratic responsibility of justice are a matter of course in a democracy, and I therefore strongly urge them to do so. There is a train in this direction, and I am convinced that it is therefore only a matter of time in Austria until we get there. We have tabled another motion for a resolution on the amendment of the Savings Act, and today we agree with your draft of the Savings Act because some of our demands are being fulfilled. There are only a few – I admit that – but there are some. We took six points back then. The first point contains the removal of the liability of the municipality for municipal savings banks. If the conversion takes place, the liability is gradually lost. The second point is the payment of liability premiums to the municipalities, which do not turn into a foundation, where new liabilities do not pass from the community to the foundation. That demand is missing! It should not be seen in a competitive credit instrument that one sector has a competitive situation other than that which is likely to pay a liability premium to its owners because of the community liability which is free of charge. I believe that this sector-specific distortion of competition can be indicated with a condition of almost one per cent. Thirdly, we have proposed to convert the lack of ownership of the association and municipal savings banks into a property of the former liability and seat communities, and to demand the privatisation of these banks fourth. I do not believe that future-oriented structures with the lack of ownership of association and municipal savings banks, such as they will continue to fit into the modern banking landscape in large areas of all those savings banks that do not turn into an AG. However, we will nevertheless give our approval to this savings bank law, because we think it is correct that the sector is combined. Sectoral cohesion means maintaining a right of pre-purchasing for a certain period of time in the sector. The sector has proved itself to be well established, whereas the structures of the savings bank sector with the lack of ownership are no longer modern in our view. Of course, if the lack of ownership of the association and municipal savings banks is removed where they are not converted into foundations, and so that the municipalities become owners in order to privatize the savings banks, a maximum proceeds of sale must be the objective for the municipalities. Those municipalities that sold their municipal savings banks actually did a very good job. An example of this is the municipality of Villach. We will agree with this novel of the Savings Act, knowing that it does not contain the breadth of our demands, but we believe that it represents a step in the right direction. Now a few remarks on Mr Graf's request from the Liberals. In my opinion, Mr Graf has made a very competent amendment to the Banking Act. This is very specific about the question of anonymity. The examination of this request has postponed the government parties by a majority in the Committee. This issue, Mr Federal Minister, we will not be able to move forward at any time. ParlaMint-AT_1998-11-05-020-XX-NRSITZ-00146_d7e10613	Mr President. High house! My venerations Ladies and gentlemen! There is no question at all that a functioning banking system is of central importance to every developed economy and that there is a wide range of legal frameworks for the exercise of this economic activity, which are internationally comparable and which are also constantly developing. On the one hand, these schemes are designed to maintain the functioning of the credit economy and to prevent the economic unreasonable behaviour of individual market participants. Of course, in every modern economic system, banking supervision must also be adapted as a system to the current development. Since spring 1998, therefore, a change in banking supervision has been underway with the aim of disseminating. These changes are also to be seen in the context of the administrative reform and are to take into account the changed conditions on the Austrian capital market. However, improvements have already been made – and the request is also very concrete in some cases. Of course, the suggestions of the 1993 Court of Auditors report have been taken up. The creation of the Federal Securities Supervisory Board has also greatly improved financial supervision in Austria. It is not, however, – and I would like to say that specifically – the banking supervision alone, which is intended to ensure the functioning of the Austrian banks and thus the protection of investors. It is a matter of the interaction of several stakeholders, who have to ensure system security in cooperation. This includes, of course, the management, the internal revision of banks, the Supervisory Board, the auditors who ultimately have to review the balance sheets and give the confirmation note, the banking supervision and, of course, the courts that have to prosecute any irregularities. Banking supervision is an economic supervision rather than police and criminal supervision. We all know that even the best rules of criminal behaviour of market participants can ultimately never be ruled out. The Rieger case – and I think you can claim it without problems – is a criminal case. The creditors of the bank have not been harmed by failed banking, but they are victims of a crime. And it was banking supervision – and I would like to see it in all its clarity – that finally revealed Rieger's crime. [[Abg. May. Stadler: The Androsch in the "Kronen-Zeitung"...!]] [[Abg. Haigermoser: The "NEWS" reporter brought him back, the Lord, from his journey!]] [[Further calls to the liberals.]] [[President Dr. Fischer gives the bell sign.]] I have Mr. Mag. We are talking, ladies and gentlemen, about criminal acts by bankers and financial service providers in the capital market, and we are talking specifically about Rieger's crime. On the one hand, we do not have these criminal investment companies, banks, investment consultants and on the other hand investors – and this is something you have to say with all clarity – who do not respect the risks, especially when high interest rates are offered. I speak here from personal experience. When consumers come to a consumer advice agency and ask if a business is serious and whether one can expect to achieve a certain interest rate, and the consultant advises them about this business and instructs them about the risk, they will still end the business. And in that I see a problem. Allow me to present a few examples. According to the Federal Securities Supervisory Board in Austria, between 30 and 40 billion Schilling disappears annually in dubious investment and securities transactions. After the Gerlach report – which you can read – it is about 70 billion D-Mark in Germany. These funds by investors disappear across borders, mostly organized in an obscene way. Trustees who are involved in these transactions are not worth the name, with this accusation referring to all occupations, which carry out or are involved in so-called charge transactions. I also call them by name: These are trade fairs, lawyers or tradesmen acting in a manner that is difficult to deal with. It's all about banks. Let me give you a concrete example. [[Abg. Böhacker: Don't generalize! That's what I'm rejecting!]] – I never generalized, I just made one statement. - Let's take Megawert from Linz. Col. Krüger knows the mega value. The megavalue has sold investment models, the prime investment program. The investors have entered into a asset management contract and then a trust agreement. There was an economic fidelity there, and everyone believed it was all right. There was nothing wrong at all, the funds disappeared in Switzerland via a Sefa AG in Liechtenstein. The investors have not received their money until today. The investors are currently represented, at least in some cases, by the Chamber of Labour, the mega value of a well-known Linz law firm. Another problem: Investment management companies organise or stage one investment fraud after another. There is Mr Brunner in Gmunden – I don't know who heard from him – who sells ownership of properties and yachts, mostly by telephone marketing from abroad, better said: he has driven them out, in the meantime he is in detention. Tax-saving loss models have been offered abroad with Austrian investors and huge interest profits have been anticipated. The whole thing was done in the snowball system. As no profits are achieved after the end of the term, they must be declared as a lover – and as it is clear, the tax axles will be recovered. Or another problem area: cross-border transactions in Switzerland or Liechtenstein. Liechtenstein, EEA member, is no longer part of the International Securities Supervisory Authority, and as far as Switzerland is concerned, we have no opportunity at all to raise the funds in that country, because there are no corresponding agreements with the European Union or between Austria and Switzerland, because Switzerland insists on a certain banking secret. According to the Consumer Information Association, the Austrian financial services market, which I mean now, is not Europe mature, it is a trap for financial charlatans. Unquestionably structural distributions and financial experts of their own graces make the development of a serious advisory structure difficult. This is the problem we have to start with when we are talking about the control of the financial market. We need to think about it: How is this financial market to be controlled? Do we need bank supervision on the one hand and, on the other hand, investment supervision or perhaps another supervisory authority on the other? Or do we confess to a uniform supervisory model? My venerations Ladies and gentlemen! In the market, banks, insurance and securities service providers are growing together. Then why not create a uniform supervision? This creates efficiency and also helps save costs. The counter-argument is the concentration of power. We will have to discuss this in detail. I personally also believe that the activity of banking supervision was absolutely in order. Banking supervision has taken action, but it has not – and has not been able to – taken over the role of justice. I personally believe that the role of justice is absolutely in need of information. Let me say this with all the clarity: The prosecutor's office – and I'm right for my colleague from the ÖVP – has to come to Trab. Just, Mr. Peter, how is this supposed to be understood to be an advertisement? Do you want to ask for instructions now, instructions that Mr Schmidt always rejects? I believe that it is necessary to ensure that all open questions are resolved by the Court of Justice. To the FPÖ still: Mr Trattner! If you look at § 3 Securities Supervisory Act, you will see what options the Securities Supervisory Board had on this question. Not at all! Because permanent emissions – and you can read this – are not subject to the prospectus requirement and do not have to be checked. [[Abg. May. Trattner: A "beautiful" gap!]] Federal Minister! Finally, the following: The economic loyalty law is currently under review; we will have to deal with the role and tasks of the economic trustees, especially when there are participations or when it comes to the issue of loyal participations. Here measures are necessary! Mr. Federal Minister, you are a sports-enthusiastic politician. The Riegerbank scandal also has something to do with Austrian sport. I would like to say this very clearly. As long as such charlatans can appear as presidents who put foreign money into an association, they distort a national competition. They're distorting the championship. I would ask you to make contact with the public authorities in your capacity as Minister of Finance and to ensure that the public authorities introduce a control procedure. Finally, I would like to say: We should consider how the financial market is being controlled more effectively. I believe that there must be uniform supervision in order to take into account investors' confidence in Austria. – Thank you for your attention.

In its current version, the Sparkassen Act was and still assumes that the individual communities are setting up such cash registers – untechnically speaking – in order to give themselves and their citizens a reliable, trusting and above all non-profit-making form of financial investments. Essential factors of this trust-building form were, on the one hand, the almost own-resourced position of the savings banks on the one hand and the yet existing failure of the municipalities on the other. ParlaMint-AT_1998-11-05-020-XX-NRSITZ-00146_d7e14659	Mr President. Mr Finanzminister! Ladies and gentlemen! There has just been a "reading" by Mr Lacner through the Savings Act. Mr Van der Bellen! I fully agree with your critical discussion, because there are serious injustices in this proposal for a revision of the Savings Act. It simply does not comply with the principle of freedom of capital. An example: There are three shareholders in a savings bank, each holding 30 percent of the shares. The first shareholder can sell his 30 percent free of charge in the capital market, to whomever he wants to offer. The second partner already has a problem. It can only sell more than 19 percent free of charge, because for the remaining 11 percent, the sector has a right of appeal. I believe that this is contrary to the principle of equality, because the one who sells as the first person comes to the advantage of selling to third parties, while the second meets the right of access on the part of the sector and cannot sell it so freely. I believe that the law should not have been so severely restricted by the right of appeal, because it will certainly come to a wave of complaints in view of the current wave of merger, a time when current shares and voting rights are being sold or sold. These actions are likely to go to the European Court of Justice. I estimate that the procedures will take four or five years each. This can certainly not be of interest to the free market economy! The second point I would like to address concerns item 8 of the agenda. This is an additional capital increase for the MIGA. I would like to deal critically with this matter, for the simple reason that there are now numerous critics of the International Monetary Fund who criticise the use of funds from capital increases. While MIGA belongs to the World Bank and not to the IMF, it can be seen under one in this context. The IMF – it criticises this very strongly – says that the funds come to countries where there are virtually no structures and that these funds do not benefit the people in need, and that they do not benefit the companies in need. The development in recent years shows quite clearly that the funds do not remain in these countries. For example, Thailand has received 18 billion dollars, Indonesia 43 billion dollars, South Korea 57 billion dollars and Russia 23 billion dollars. [[Federal Minister Edlinger: But not by the MIGA!]] – Not from the MIGA, but from the Monetary Fund; I have already said that. The problem is, and you know it very well, Minister of Finance, that these amounts do not remain in the country. These funds flow back abroad and serve only to keep banks that have committed themselves there harmless. But the population does not benefit. The MIGA situation is as follows: The capital increase is currently back to $1 billion. And even with the MIGA there are no relevant directives, for example in the way one says: First, the structures in the country concerned must be changed before we give guarantees for, so to speak, non-commercial risks. – Therefore, practically everyone can come and say: I have a non-commercial risk. It's no matter what project I invest in, where I move my money – it doesn't matter, it's always guaranteed. That's the wrong way! First, the structures need to be changed in order to provide a secure framework for financing investment projects. Only then should we be prepared to talk about development aid for the country concerned. But before the structures are changed there, Minister of Finance, you will not agree with us that we will transfer money there and will not know what will happen to it.

One thesis states that declining development aid is offset by rising investment in the Third World. This thesis is not sustainable to 100 percent. Although there has been an enormous expansion of direct investment in the Third World, 75 percent of all such direct investment is concentrated on only 15 countries, and 50 percent of all direct investment is on a single country, namely China. That is to say, there is no very large diversification of these direct investments, and there are around 85 countries in the Third World who should and would like to take advantage of these investments much more than they have been. Mr Trattner, on a point of order: Of course, developing countries too have an essential role to play in ensuring that investments can take place in a reasonable manner by changing the framework conditions. At the same time, however, such a guarantee instrument such as the MIGA really offers the opportunity to motivate international investors to invest in individual countries in a start-up phase. I think, especially if we have a relatively critical attitude to development aid, as is the case with the FPÖ, we should actually fail to support private economic support instruments, which have partially replaced development aid. I therefore believe that the position of the FPÖ or, at least, of Mr Trattner on this point is not very consistent, because either one decides to say: We want a great increase in traditional development aid to help these countries, or we decide to give complementary protection guarantees for development aid, so that private investment can progress accordingly. If one wants to do so, then one must agree to this increase of MIGA in this very modest form, because it is actually one of the most successful instruments within the framework of the entire international financial institutions. Thank you.

So far, so good – or so far, so bad. Now I come to practice, Minister of State. As tobacco tax legislation regulates the movement of tobacco products from other EU Member States, in practice a wholesaler will have to have warehouses in Austria for tax reasons alone. So they decide a law that takes up something without it in practice at all. I think there is something to overrun the pre-emptive obedience to the EU.

The universality of human rights is not only an objective state of action, it is also subjectively accepted by humanity. However, at the end of this process, not only should recognised human rights ideas stand, but above all effective guarantees against states that violate them. Only the extension of the protection of human rights to all States while at the same time safeguarding by a national authority, namely the State community, will bring improvements. Unfortunately, there is no monopoly of violence at international level, as it marks the emergence of modern states. ParlaMint-AT_1998-11-05-020-XX-NRSITZ-00146_d7e17756	Mr Dr. Maitz has been reported to speak. Please, honourable Member.
ParlaMint-AT_1998-11-05-020-XX-NRSITZ-00146_d7e17778	Mr President. Dear Mr. Vice-Chancellor! Ladies and gentlemen! The representatives of the People's Party support all initiatives that make the work of the Austrian Federal Government effective in the field of international peacekeeping. In this context, the agreement between Austria and Denmark on the status of the United Nations multinational brigade is highly willing to mention what we are negotiating here today and what we are going to decide on. It is about legal status, equipment and forms of cooperation for those Austrians – including Austrians in the future – who are currently working on this brigade or in the future. The aim is to halve the lead period from eight weeks to about four weeks for future peacekeeping operations. The suggestion came in 1995 from the then Secretary General of the United Nations, Boutros Boutros-Ghali. The Danish Minister for Defence Haekkerup has taken up this initiative and has been committed to the establishment of the multinational rapid intervention force for peacekeeping and maintenance measures. That is why the headquarters of the Brigadestabe is in Denmark, Copenhagen. Austria, together with seven other countries, is participating in the planning work and has been entrusted with relevant functions which I would like to mention. First: Lieutenant Gerald Hatzenbichler is a liaison officer between the military, the civil authorities and the non-governmental organizations – a diplomatically high-quality task for a talented Austrian officer. Secondly, Major Adolf Brückler is responsible for operational planning. He comes from the pioneer field and is therefore a particularly suitable candidate for this task. As of mid-2000, Austria will be calling on the head of the Planning Department to work under this SHIRBRIG, a brigade that will be quickly called into action in peacekeeping, peacekeeping and peacekeeping operations. Members of the Federal Force take up this task because we can bring in experience from 38 years of UN efforts for peace and security in Europe and the world and because our positions can be reviewed in an international comparison. How do we stand in the case of training, equipment, procedure planning and organisation of these operations? So far, the achievements of the Austrian federal army and its soldiers in foreign operations have always been assessed positively. But that does not mean that we should not learn and be able to learn. Ladies and gentlemen! Since 1960, 38 000 members of the army have been active in peace missions within international organizations in 38 years. We are convinced that participation in these peace-supporting operations is also in the greatest interest of the Austrian population and will therefore be in agreement with the present agreement.

ParlaMint-AT_1998-11-05-020-XX-NRSITZ-00146_d7e17966	President. — I call the Group of the European People's Party (Christian Democrats), who have spoken on behalf of the Group of the Please, ladies and gentlemen.
ParlaMint-AT_1998-11-05-020-XX-NRSITZ-00146_d7e17988	Mr President. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! The agreements, which are now before us, are mainly agreements to intensify and improve international cooperation. The agreement between Austria and the Russian Federation aims to substantially strengthen and promote scientific and technical cooperation, which already exists between universities, institutes and other research institutions. Special weight is placed, among other things, on the exchange of scientists and experts. A special feature of the new agreement is reciprocity on the one hand. In other words, the contributions of the Contracting States should be balanced financially and in terms of content in the future. What is important and new, on the other hand, is the explicitly desired reference to EU research projects and multilateral programmes. What is important to us in Austrian foreign policy is realized in and with this seemingly unspectacular agreement: Stable, sustainable relations with other countries are built and promoted by cultural and scientific cooperation, joint projects and the development of common interests. This cooperation is also an expression of the conviction that we can only shape international co-existence in equal opportunities at all levels of life. This willingness to cooperate equally corresponds to the Additional Agreement on the Headquarters of the International Atomic Energy Agency, which increases the opportunities of staff members to gain better access to the Austrian labour market. What now applies to most international organisations should now also apply to the International Atomic Energy Agency. In my view, the present addition to the agreement is particularly welcome in the interests of women: Firstly, the seemingly neutral concept of "members" mainly refers to women whose professional mobility, career and economic independence were limited by the rules in force so far, and secondly, good working conditions for members also facilitate the careers of women in international organisations, because members are reluctant to give up their self-employed work in favour of the foreign career of their partners. – I believe that this agreement is also important. I am not going closer to the agreement between Austria and Denmark now, because it has already been discussed intensively anyway. While the above-mentioned agreements focus on intensifying the relationship and improving the status of people in a foreign country, the Treaty announcement with South Africa is about a change in the Treaty which takes account of the Schengen Agreement. That is to say, for Austria, the need to agree or harmonise the visa and entry provisions with the Schengen partners. From my point of view, this schematic procedure across all states is not quite unproblemable, and therefore my colleague Irmtraut Karlsson will put forward a motion for a resolution to encourage a differentiated political approach to the issue of visas. With this addition, in other words with this motion for a resolution, my group agrees with the current government proposals. - Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_1998-11-05-020-XX-NRSITZ-00146_d7e18104	Mrs Gredler. — (DE) Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, I should like to speak on behalf of the Group of the European People's Party. Voluntary speaking time limit: 3 minutes. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_1998-11-05-020-XX-NRSITZ-00146_d7e18126	Mr President. Mr. Vice-Chancellor! Ladies and gentlemen! I would like to express my views on two areas. Firstly, to the Additional Agreement with the Atomic Energy Agency. Of course, you can view this as a privilege and ask the question: Is it justified that family members of those who work in international organizations are given special access to and preferred to the labour market? – It is a life partner or the children. All other persons are excluded. I believe that there should be a discussion about privileges and immunities in general, not only in Austria, but also in all the countries concerned. The question should be asked whether the immunities that we grant are still necessary in the present day. This was once the right way to protect diplomats and other similar personnel. However, I dare to doubt that this protection is really necessary to this extent today. In this context, I think in particular of the family. Most of them are women who have to give up their careers so that they can make a family life even if, as it is often, the husband is active in an international organization and they have to leave home. I would also very much like this preference for spouses of diplomats from Austria who are looking for work in other countries, so that they do not have to get into the end of the chain and only after four years receive a work permit, but that the difficult situation in which they are located is taken into account when they move to another country. I don't think that's unfair. But again: The entire debate on privilege and immunity should be held worldwide, especially in the European countries. That would be a good approach. On the points of view and South Africa: I regret that the interpretation of the Schengen Treaty goes in the direction that it is necessary to succeed in doing so, i.e. removing a fee. Article 9 of the Schengen Treaty reads as follows: "The Parties undertake to pursue a common policy on the movement of persons, in particular with regard to the rules of sight. ... The Contracting Parties undertake to continue to harmonise their view-making policy in agreement." I would think that this part of the text does not address fees. I would even like us to break our heads on how we can give visas free of charge all over the world. In my opinion, this would be the right approach in a mobile world of communication!

ParlaMint-AT_1998-11-05-020-XX-NRSITZ-00146_d7e18199	Mr President. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen. Firstly, I would also like to refer to the announcement of the agreement between Austria and South Africa or the introduction of visa fees. This has been mentioned more often today, and I too would like to address it with a few words. We have already discussed in the committee the fact that the bilateral agreement that existed between the Republic of South Africa and Austria, which has granted free movement between these two countries, is of course not a good idea, and we are now, as the apartheid regime has been abolished and the country is on a democratic path, increasing the conditions. I see this similar to my previous speaker, Mrs Gredler. I too cannot read from the Schengen Agreement at the best that the equalisation of visas must also necessarily be the equivalent of the fees. There is now this joint proposal, which will still be presented, to which we agree. I think it is a common way to discuss this issue in the Schengen Executive Committee. I would just like to say so much: I would like us to set ourselves a time limit for the Federal Minister to report on whether this proposal has been put forward, how it has been received, how the others have responded to it. I think that this is useful with regard to this request. I am pleased that what we have debated in the Committee on the agenda has obviously found that there is now this joint request. The fact that there is a request then confirms to me that some discussion in the Committee is not entirely in vain. In the second part of my speech, I refer to the SHIRBRIG agreement, which has also been referred to. I must say that when I listened to my previous speakers, especially Maitz and Jung, I asked myself whether I was right with my opinion. I rarely agree with these two colleagues on the adoption of an item on the agenda, but I do not agree with them in the argument that will calm me down again. Nevertheless, I would like to say a few things about this because I just want to see it differently and to explain it differently than my two previous speakers. We welcome and support this agreement. This may be surprising because, as I believe, you have never really understood what our peace-making options for Europe or our global peace-making options are all about. If, on the one hand, we are clearly in favour of disarmament also of the Austrian federal army and still believe that there is still a reduction compatible and even necessary, this does not mean that on the other hand we do not consider it necessary to come to such multinational forces and brigades. However, the main difference to what has been discussed so far in this House is that this has to be done very clearly within the framework of the United Nations. That was always our desire and our effort to make clear: We need such a thing, but we need it within the framework of the United Nations. It then looks quite different, not only because there are clear decisions and orders – at least I assume – by the United Nations, but because, in our opinion, the UN is the only legitimate international body responsible for this. What we have always criticized so far was that there has often been a wild growth: NATO has decided to intervene somewhere, in some cases there have been very little viable, even partly no decisions of the Security Council, in some cases it has not even occurred to NATO interventions, but to some cross-border actions. That, we think, is not a good way, especially then – and we are now in a foreign policy debate – when it comes to a peaceful foreign policy perspective. If peacekeeping takes place within the framework of a foreign policy approach, a foreign policy procedure, this is, in our view, a really different approach. In that respect, I would like to be quite clear about what my previous speakers have said. [[Congratulation with the greens.]] I am not at all concerned with facilitating the work of the federal army. Rather, I was concerned with questioning the German army on the issue of what is actually to be done internationally and what we can or cannot do. I do not know whether Austria should necessarily bring in the German army, as it is now, as it might be or as it is not, – let us put it down – or whether Austria could not bring in a completely different performance, a completely different logistics of peacekeeping, which would be very, very essential. I can tell you that at the various international meetings, seminars or conferences, I am constantly being asked about the contribution made by Austria with the University of Peace in Schlaining and the training that takes place there. That's where we play an excellent role – internationally considered, internationally respected. So it may be – and I am this view – quite different logistical areas that we should bring into such international peacekeeping operations. Finally, I would like to say two or three words about peacekeeping: I think that we must not lose sight of the fact that the classic United Nations operations, such as they take place in Cyprus, at the Golan Heights and in other areas, continue to have their importance and importance. I would like to say here and in this debate that I do not support all the considerations that are being taken up in the Ministry of Defence, that I am withdrawing the forces there, and that I am more involved in the rapid intervention force. I mean, we have had very good experiences and good work in this area of peacekeeping. This should not now be questioned or even cancelled. Secondly, I would like to say to peacekeeping: I would like us to be able to do more preventive work in this area. With regard to the importance of preventive work for me, I would like to draw on a specific example: If today, with all legitimate concerns, we are discussing the fact that 2 000 OSCE observers will soon be stationed in Kosovo, then I think we should be ready to discuss and consider whether it will be urgently necessary to station such observers in Macedonia, for example, in order to promote understanding of the need to extend a mission there both to the OSCE and to the UN in order to prevent a situation which I believe is preemptive in the light of the elections in Macedonia. In my opinion, the situation I would like to address very briefly goes in a direction which is very, very worrying. [[Interference of Vice-Chancellor Dr. Schüssel.]] I know there are both the UN and the OSCE. The only question is whether that will be enough. For I do not share your view, Mr Federal Minister, nor do I share the opinion of the bourgeois newspaper "Die Presse" that the results of the elections in Macedonia will bring a calming down of the situation. I fear, rather, that there will be a very strong concern about the situation. Two very radical national parties have won an election there, and a coalition between the VRMO and the DPA is characterised. From my experience I can only say: If two radical, very nationalist parties come together, then the option is that each of the two parties will probably follow their path, which will not necessarily lead to the unity of Macedonia, as we know from the past. [[Abg. Smolle: The report in the "Wiener Zeitung", on which you base yourself, does not represent it properly!]] That's not true! I know the situation in Macedonia and can certainly judge it! And that in an area in which there has already been a very large instability or a very fragile ceasefire. In order to return to the real concern: For me, in the area of peacekeeping, it is rather a question of focusing more on these preventive possibilities or options, in order to get away step by step from the need for rapid intervention troops, which can only always prevent the worst or the worst, but can in no way really contribute to a medium-term or longer-term peace solution. Thank you.

Sometimes it occurs that in the drive of politics a good idea or a good measure in administrative business is not fully implemented in the intended sense. This also concerns the Government's proposal to terminate the bilateral agreement with South Africa on freedom of charge. So well, as my previous speaker said, the idea of a large area in Europe is due to the Schengen Agreement, in which there are no borders and no border controls, so careful must be handled so that it does not come to a fortress Schengen, and we are simply administrative – and this should have been the case here – to close everything out, to the conclusion that the Executive Council has decided, but rather a political but also a cultural – on which I would like to insist – discussion takes place on what relations with some countries exist. I would like to give an example from the bilateral visa history of Austria: Austria had visas and fees with the small Caribbean state of St. Lucia with 200 000 inhabitants. Why? – The Archaeological Institute of the University of Vienna carried out interesting ethnological and archaeological excavations there, and there was to some extent also tourism with that state. That is why this agreement has been reached for the mutual relief of this agreement. This was not a contract – that is why we did not have to cancel it – but only a change of notes, which provided for visas and free of charge. Similar things should also be possible in this larger Europe of the Schengen States, such as, as Mr Vice-Chancellor said in the Committee, Estonia or the other two Baltic countries, which are not yet candidates for accession. We also need to consider whether we need to close the bulkheads. That is why it is so important that this motion for a resolution, which I have already mentioned three times, will also be adopted. It says: [[Quoted print version removed]] But I do not want to let this item on the agenda pass - my predecessor said that Europe's security is guaranteed by these police measures of the Schengen Agreement - without pointing out that the police alone do not guarantee security. There are more reports and opinions which make it clear that security risks in Europe are quite different, namely that nuclear weapons are sometimes turned off and the whole system is still as if we had two blocks and the Cold War. Next week, a treaty for a nuclear-free world and a new disarmament agenda will be presented at the UN. From Europe, Ireland and Sweden are leading, New Zealand, the Republic of South Africa and several Latin American states are also involved. I hope that, as we are concerned about the security of this world, Austria will not only be able to work in this resolution, but will be actively involved in it. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_1998-11-05-020-XX-NRSITZ-00146_d7e18740	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! One could say to enlargement: nothing new. There were three enlargement rounds at least. The first time around England, Denmark and Ireland, the second time around Spain, Portugal and Greece, the third time around Austria, Sweden and Finland. And yet a lot of new and difficult things are there. For the first time, negotiations with post-communist countries have to be conducted. Why is this so difficult? This is not only about the acquis communautaire, but also about the legal system that everyone has to take on, especially on fundamental legal issues, because if we are not a Community here, it is not very useful – but also about economic issues and many other problems. But the ideological brainwashing is still added to these countries. For years and decades, they were weaned to appreciate the freedom to make decisions. There are always those examples from the practice that someone says: I would like the Ministry of Export to determine my export quota, not me as a manager. You can't change that from today to tomorrow. The question of consciousness is a long-term issue, and it will burden us for a long time within the European Union. We have the difficulty of taking over the communist heritage at all, be it Chernobyl, whether it be the catastrophic ecological conditions in certain areas of the post-Communist countries or the many other problems, such as the low competitiveness of the economy. All of this makes the question much more difficult. You can't say: Portugal was also well below the average of the European Union's economic performance. But it would be wrong to always see the difficulties. However, one must realistically see them, one must deal with them. Ladies and gentlemen! Why are we for that? – Because we are interested in a Europe that has no lines of break and that we have borders with neighbours who are as stable as we are. The stability of Hungary, the Slovenians and the other neighbours is partly also our stability. Peace demands stability, and that is why we want these countries to be there too. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] They must make exactly the same structural changes that we had to make, and many more because of their history. Only then will they form economically and socially powerful units. Then there will be these sharper competitions between us, but also the stability that I spoke about earlier. Ladies and gentlemen, I think that must be our goal, especially Austria's objective. One of our functions has always been to make a bridge. There is no need to argue about neutrality here, because I believe that our geographical position requires that we also form a bridge to these countries. Not least, there is a great deal of expectation among these countries. It was not only out of special kindness that the European Union, or the European Commission, as the body that was then important in our opinion stated that there would be another European policy in the south-east of Europe if a country like Austria were a member, having experience in living with these peoples, with these peoples, who know them well, which is well known there – despite all the difficulties that history has shown. I think we should go this path in our interest, even if the conditions – I stress this again – can be very difficult. For example, the change in the common agricultural policy – there are few things that are more difficult than the solution to this question – would be tax policy, institutional reform. There can not only be a "widening". Quantitative thinking alone never leads to quality in society. We also need "depending" to strengthen political integration in order to keep it efficient. To be clear, the only argument that would be against me would be to "widening", only to multiply, without improving the performance and decision-making capacity. This cannot be our goal, which we take seriously in a common Europe. This can and will also lead to discussions here. But let's just think back: All of this has been done in recent years. It's going to be a disaster if we're going to be a member, it's called. It will be a disaster for our gold reserves. It will be a disaster if we join monetary union. – We all mastered it, even if there were occasional difficulties. I stick to my conviction: The Austrians are better than they occasionally accept this from themselves. You just need to look at the story. This is, I believe, the only way in which we can make a credible contribution on the issue of enlargement, which corresponds to our interests, which corresponds to the interests of the neighbours and their interests, and which serves the European peace that we all strive for.

Instead of discussing how we can support this enlargement as positively as possible and accompany it politically, show the superiority and say: We don't need that, we already have that, thanks, we don't want to accept it!

ParlaMint-AT_1998-11-05-020-XX-NRSITZ-00146_d7e19498	Mr President. Mr. Vice-Chancellor! Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! I believe that the central political objective of enlargement should not be disputed that the best contribution to the stabilisation of the pan-European continent is, of course, the enlargement of the European Union. I mean, if we start from this confession, we can discuss the conditions under which this Easter enlargement should take place. In this regard, the European Union has decided on a realistic procedure, which is also reflected in the fact that it is not expected until all the parts of the Acquis screening are finished, but that, for example, already on 10 October. Negotiations will begin in Vienna on the first concrete chapters under the Austrian Presidency. This does not lead to a loss of time, and we can discuss in concrete terms the problems that are on the agenda in this context. Another point, I think, is essential: There is always talk about the question of global economic comparisons. I consider this approach to be too brief. Because it is not crucial whether all of Poland, whole Hungary or the whole of the Czech Republic have the same or similar economic data as Austria or Germany. Instead, it is crucial whether there is regional economic development at the borders, which makes the transition from one country to another more harmonious. What do I mean, for example? – European integration does not stop, for example, that the unemployment rate in southern Spain is unfortunately still 20 percent. It was far more important that there was an economic development in northern Spain, Catalonia, which has led to the de facto absence of economic differences between southern France and northern Spain and thus a possible combination with all the positive consequences. There has been no migration movement or escape to the place where there would be higher wages or similar. If we look at this differently, we can see that there are already very different developments in the Eastern European countries in the preparations for enlargement. For example, see the economic situation in Westungarn: This is significantly different from the development of those areas that are farther away from the European Union. I therefore believe that it is necessary to think much more about how the new cross-border spaces can be designed. In this context, I would also like to say quite openly something about the current border regions of Austria. [[Abg. Ing. Nut trees: It would also be too short if you did not involve agriculture!]] Mr Nußbaumer! Let's talk openly! For decades, we have argued that the development disadvantage, for example of the forest and wine district, is that it lies on the dead border, on the iron curtain and therefore there is no economic hinterland. If the issue of enlargement of the East is now on the agenda, we must be aware that only through the enlargement of the East there is the possibility that the border areas of Austria will also be given the opportunity to become cross-border economic growth together with the strongest growth poles in the East. That is why I believe that sometimes fears are startled on the basis of misconceptions, which are in fact not in the well-understood interest of Austria. For with all the political significance of Eastern enlargement for Europe as a whole, it must be perfectly clear that one of the main beneficiaries will also be political, economic and long-term social Austria. In the future, Austria will be in the heart of a Europe that is growing together with all economic advantages, and this is the central point in the political debate!
